[India](14533.md) has a [developing](78449.md) [market-oriented economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy) with substantial state participation in strategic sectors.[^cite_note-50] It is the [world's sixth-largest economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) by [nominal GDP](12594.md) and the third-largest by [purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity) (PPP) as of April 2026.[^cite_note-ID-6][^cite_note-51] On a [per capita income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_capita_income) basis, the nation is ranked [149th by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)_per_capita) and [119th by PPP-adjusted GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_per_capita_\(PPP\)) as of 2026. From [independence in 1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_independence_movement) until 1991, economic development was characterized by [protectionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism) economic policies, with extensive [state intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_intervention), [demand-side economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demand-side_economics), natural resource optimization, and regulation. An acute [balance of payments crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Indian_economic_crisis) in 1991 led to the adoption of broad economic liberalisation in India and [indicative planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirigisme).

Nearly 70% of India's GDP is driven by domestic consumption, with the nation consistently the world's [third-largest consumer market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_consumer_markets). As of 2025, the service sector accounts for around 55% of GDP.[^cite_note-52] Aside from private consumption, India's GDP is fueled by government spending, investments, and exports.[^cite_note-53] As of 2025, India is the world's [7th-largest importer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_imports) and the [10th-largest exporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_exports).[^cite_note-54] India is often described as the "pharmacy of the world", supplying around one-fifth of global demand for pharmaceuticals to over 200 countries.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56] India is the largest vaccine manufacturer globally by volume, accounting for over 60% of the world's production.[^cite_note-57] India is the world's [fifth-largest manufacturer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manufacturing#List_of_countries_by_manufacturing_output), representing 3.2% of global manufacturing output.[^cite_note-auto2-58] India's digital economy was estimated to be 11.7% of GDP, with its total value expected to surpass US\$1 trillion by 2029.[^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60] Nearly 63% of [India's population lives in rural areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_India), and generates about 46% of Indian GDP.[^cite_note-61][^cite_note-62]

India has been a member of the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) (WTO) since 1995.[^cite_note-63] It ranks 41st on the [Global Competitiveness Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WEF_Global_Competitiveness_Report) and 39th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index).[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65] As of 2025, India ranks third in the world in [total number of billionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_billionaires).[^cite_note-66] India's [Gini coefficient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gini_coefficient) fell to 25.5 by 2023, making it the fourth-most equal country globally, suggesting significant progress in income equality.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68] Economists and social scientists often consider India a [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state).[^cite_note-India_kapur-69][^cite_note-India_aspalter-70] India's overall [social welfare spending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_security_in_India) stood at 8.6% of GDP in 2022.[^cite_note-71] It has two of the world's ten [largest stock exchanges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Largest_stock_exchanges). India has free-trade agreements with many world nations, including the [European Union](9317.md), and is a member of numerous economic organisations. India is a [lower-middle income](78449.md) country with much of its [modern economic development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_development_in_India) focused on improving [standards of living](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_of_living) for its population, which has been the [largest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population) since the mid-2020s. The accuracy of Indian economic and inflation data has been challenged by the [IMF](15251.md) for not suitably incorporating the [informal sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_economy) and [consumption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumption_\(economics\)).[^cite_note-d952-72]

## History

Main articles: [Economic history of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_India) and [Timeline of the economy of the Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_economy_of_the_Indian_subcontinent)



[Aurangzeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurangzeb) expanded the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) and made it the region with largest GDP in the 17th century.

[Indus Valley Civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_Civilisation), the early civilisation of [India](14533.md) and [Pakistan](23235.md), developed the economy of agriculture and craft which later spread into central India.[^cite_note-u759-73] [Angus Maddison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angus_Maddison) estimates that from 1-1000 AD, the regions making up the present-day India contributed roughly 30% of the world's population and GDP.[^cite_note-maddison2612-74]

India experienced per-capita [GDP growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP_growth) in the [high medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_India) era.[^cite_note-75] By the late 17th century, most of the Indian subcontinent had been united under the [Mughal Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) [Aurangzeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurangzeb), which for a time Maddison estimates became the largest economy and manufacturing power in the world, producing about a quarter of global GDP, before fragmenting and being conquered over the next century.[^cite_note-Maddisson_World_GDP-76] The combination of [protectionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionist), [import-substitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Import-substitution), [Fabian socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fabian_Society), and [social democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_democracy)-inspired policies governed India for sometime after the end of [British rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj). The economy was then characterised as [Dirigism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirigisme),[^cite_note-PE-77][^cite_note-BB-78] due to extensive regulation, [protectionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism), and [public ownership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_ownership) of large monopolies.[^cite_note-oecd-79][^cite_note-nyt-80][^cite_note-potential-81] Since 1991, [continuing economic liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalisation_in_India) has moved the country towards a [market-based economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy).[^cite_note-oecd-79][^cite_note-nyt-80] By 2008, India had established itself as one of the world's faster-growing economies.

### Ancient and medieval eras

#### Indus Valley Civilisation

The citizens of the [Indus Valley civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_civilisation), a permanent settlement that flourished between 2800 BCE and 1800 BCE, practised agriculture, domesticated animals, used uniform weights and measures, made tools and weapons, and traded with other cities. Evidence of well-planned streets, a drainage system, and [water supply](684928.md) reveals their knowledge of [urban planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_planning), which included the first-known urban [sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitation) systems and the existence of a form of municipal government.[^cite_note-Discovery-1-82]

#### West Coast

Maritime trade was carried out extensively between [southern regions of India](678583.md) and [Southeast Asia](28741.md) and West Asia from early times until around the fourteenth century CE. Both the [Malabar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malabar_Coast) and [Coromandel Coasts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coromandel_Coast) were the sites of important trading centres from as early as the first century BCE, used for import and export as well as transit points between the [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean) region and southeast Asia.[^cite_note-83] Over time, traders organised themselves into associations which received state patronage. This state patronage for overseas trade came to an end by the thirteenth century CE, when it was largely taken over by the local Parsi, Jewish, Syrian Christian, and Muslim communities, initially on the Malabar and subsequently on the Coromandel coast.[^cite_note-84]

#### Silk Route

Other scholars suggest trading from India to West Asia and Eastern Europe was active between the 14th and 18th centuries.[^cite_note-hanway1753-85][^cite_note-dale2002-86][^cite_note-levi2002-87] During this period, Indian traders settled in [Surakhani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suraxan%C4%B1_raion), a suburb of greater [Baku](4566.md), Azerbaijan. These traders built a [Hindu temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ateshgah_of_Baku), which suggests commerce was active and prosperous for Indians by the 17th century.[^cite_note-jackson1911-88][^cite_note-forster1798-89][^cite_note-morier1818-90][^cite_note-usgovt1887hd-91]

Further north, the [Saurashtra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saurashtra_\(region\)) and [Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal) coasts played an important role in maritime trade, and the [Gangetic plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangetic_plains) and the [Indus valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_valley) housed several centres of river-borne commerce. Most overland trade was carried out via the [Khyber Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khyber_Pass) connecting the [Punjab region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab_region) with Afghanistan and onward to the Middle East and Central Asia.[^cite_note-92] Although many kingdoms and rulers issued coins, [barter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barter) was prevalent. Villages paid a portion of their agricultural produce as revenue to the rulers, while their craftsmen received a part of the crops at harvest time for their services.[^cite_note-Dutt-1-93]



Silver coin of the [Maurya Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurya_Empire), 3rd century BCE



Silver coin of the [Gupta dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_dynasty), 5th century CE

### Mughal, Rajput, and Maratha eras (1526–1820)

See also: [Muslin trade in Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslin_trade_in_Bengal) and [Economy of the Kingdom of Mysore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Kingdom_of_Mysore)

The Indian economy was the largest and most prosperous throughout world history and would continue to be under the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire), up until the 18th century.[^cite_note-schmidt-94] Sean Harkin estimates that China and India may have accounted for 60 to 70 percent of world GDP in the 17th century. The Mughal economy functioned on an elaborate system of [coined](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coin) currency, land revenue and trade. Gold, silver and copper coins were issued by the royal [mints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mint_\(coin\)) which functioned on the basis of [free coinage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commodity_money).[^cite_note-95] The political stability and uniform revenue policy resulting from a centralized administration under the Mughals, coupled with a well-developed internal trade network, ensured that India–before the arrival of the British–was to a large extent economically unified, despite having a traditional agrarian economy characterised by a predominance of [subsistence agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsistence_agriculture).[^cite_note-96] Agricultural production increased under Mughal [agrarian reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarian_reform),[^cite_note-schmidt-94] with Indian agriculture being advanced compared to Europe at the time, such as the widespread use of the [seed drill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seed_drill) among Indian peasants before its adoption in European agriculture,[^cite_note-97] and possibly higher per-capita agricultural output and standards of consumption than 17th century Europe.[^cite_note-98]

The Mughal Empire had a thriving industrial manufacturing economy, with India producing about 25% of the world's industrial output up until 1750,[^cite_note-williamson-99] making it the most important manufacturing centre in [international trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_trade).[^cite_note-WE-100] Manufactured goods and [cash crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cash_crop) from the Mughal Empire were sold throughout the world. Key industries included textiles, [shipbuilding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shipbuilding), and steel, and processed exports included cotton textiles, [yarns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarn), [thread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thread_\(yarn\)), silk, [jute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jute) products, [metalware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metalware), and foods such as sugar, oils and butter.[^cite_note-schmidt-94] Cities and towns boomed under the Mughal Empire, which had a relatively high degree of urbanization for its time, with 15% of its population living in urban centres, higher than the percentage of the urban population in contemporary Europe at the time and higher than that of [British India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_India) in the 19th century.[^cite_note-eraly-101]

In [early modern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_Europe), there was significant demand for products from Mughal India, particularly cotton textiles, as well as goods such as spices, peppers, [indigo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigo), silks, and [saltpeter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saltpeter) (for use in [munitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munitions)).[^cite_note-schmidt-94] [European fashion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1650%E2%80%931700_in_Western_European_fashion), for example, became increasingly dependent on Mughal Indian textiles and silks. From the late 17th century to the early 18th century, Mughal India accounted for 95% of [British imports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company) from Asia, and the [Bengal Subah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Subah) province alone accounted for 40% of [Dutch imports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company) from Asia.[^cite_note-Prakash-102] In contrast, there was very little demand for European goods in Mughal India, which was largely self-sufficient.[^cite_note-schmidt-94] Indian goods, especially those from Bengal, were also exported in large quantities to other Asian markets, such as [Indonesia](14579.md) and Japan.[^cite_note-richards95-103] At the time, [Mughal Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Bengal) was the most important centre of cotton textile production.[^cite_note-104]

In the early 18th century the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) declined, as it lost western, central and parts of south and north India to the [Maratha Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maratha_Empire), which integrated and continued to administer those regions.[^cite_note-kumar-105] The decline of the Mughal Empire led to decreased agricultural productivity, which in turn negatively affected the textile industry.[^cite_note-106] The subcontinent's dominant economic power in the post-Mughal era was the [Bengal Subah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Subah) in the east., which continued to maintain thriving textile industries and relatively high [real wages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_wages).[^cite_note-Parthasarathi-107] However, the former was devastated by the [Maratha invasions of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maratha_invasions_of_Bengal)[^cite_note-Chaudhuri253-108][^cite_note-Marshall73-109] and then [British colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Plassey) in the mid-18th century.[^cite_note-Parthasarathi-107] After the loss at the [Third Battle of Panipat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Battle_of_Panipat), the Maratha Empire disintegrated into several confederate states, and the resulting political instability and armed conflict severely affected economic life in several parts of the country – although this was mitigated by localised prosperity in the new provincial kingdoms.[^cite_note-kumar-105] By the late eighteenth century, the British [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company) had entered the Indian political theatre and established its dominance over other European powers. This marked a determinative shift in India's trade, and a less-powerful effect on the rest of the economy.[^cite_note-110]

### British era (1793–1947)

Main articles: [Economy of India under Company rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_India_under_Company_rule) and [Economy of India under the British Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_India_under_the_British_Raj)

> There is no doubt that our grievances against the British Empire had a sound basis. As the painstaking statistical work of the Cambridge historian Angus Maddison has shown, India's share of world income collapsed from 22.6% in 1700, almost equal to Europe's share of 23.3% at that time, to as low as 3.8% in 1952. Indeed, at the beginning of the 20th century, "the brightest jewel in the British Crown" was the poorest country in the world in terms of per capita income.

— [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh)

[^cite_note-Hindu-111]

From the beginning of the 19th century, the British [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company)'s gradual expansion and consolidation of power brought a major change in taxation and agricultural policies, which tended to promote commercialisation of agriculture with a focus on trade, resulting in decreased production of food crops, mass impoverishment and destitution of farmers, and in the short term, led to [numerous famines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Famine_in_India).[^cite_note-112] The economic policies of the [British Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj) caused a severe decline in the [handicrafts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handicrafts) and [handloom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handloom) sectors, due to reduced demand and dipping employment.[^cite_note-113] After the removal of international restrictions by the [Charter of 1813](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company#Charter_Act_1813), Indian trade expanded substantially with steady growth.[^cite_note-114] The result was a significant transfer of capital from India to Britain, which, due to the colonial policies of the British, led to a massive drain of revenue rather than any systematic effort at modernisation of the domestic economy.[^cite_note-115] The economy of the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent) was the [largest in the world for most of recorded history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_regions_by_past_GDP_\(PPP\)) up until the onset of [colonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_India) in early 19th century.[^cite_note-Maddison379-116][^cite_note-maddison261-117][^cite_note-118]



Estimated GDP per capita of India and United Kingdom during 1700–1950 in 1990 US\$ according to [Maddison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angus_Maddison).

[^cite_note-119]

&#x20;Maddison's estimates for 18th-century India have been criticized as gross underestimates,

[^cite_note-120]

&#x20;[Bairoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Bairoch) estimates India had a higher GDP per capita in the 18th century,

[^cite_note-121]

[^cite_note-122]

&#x20;and Parthasarathi's findings show higher [real wages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_wages) in 18th-century [Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Subah) and [Mysore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Mysore).

[^cite_note-Parthasarathi38-123]

[^cite_note-williamson-99]

&#x20;There is consensus that India's per capita GDP and income stagnated during the colonial era, starting in the late 18th century.

[^cite_note-Kumar-3-124]

Under British rule, India's share of the world economy declined from 24.4% in 1700 down to 4.2% in 1950. India's GDP (PPP) per capita was stagnant during the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) and began to decline prior to the onset of British rule.[^cite_note-maddison261-117] India's share of global industrial output declined from 25% in 1750 down to 2% in 1900.[^cite_note-williamson-99] At the same time, Britain's share of the world economy rose from 2.9% in 1700 up to 9% in 1870. The British East India Company, following their [conquest of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Plassey) in 1757, had forced open the large Indian market to British goods, which could be sold in India without [tariffs](55551.md) or [duties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duty_\(economics\)), compared to local Indian producers who were heavily taxed, while in Britain [protectionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionist) policies such as bans and high tariffs were implemented to restrict Indian textiles from being sold there, whereas raw cotton was imported from India without tariffs to British factories which manufactured textiles from Indian cotton and sold them back to the Indian market. British economic policies gave them a monopoly over India's large market and cotton resources.[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-gupta-126][^cite_note-127] India served as both a significant supplier of raw goods to British manufacturers and a large [captive market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captive_market) for British manufactured goods.[^cite_note-128]

British territorial expansion in India throughout the 19th century created an institutional environment that, on paper, guaranteed [property rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_rights) among the colonisers, encouraged [free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade), and created a single currency with [fixed exchange rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fixed_exchange_rates), standardised weights and measures and [capital markets](41914.md) within the company-held territories. It also established a system of [railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_India) and telegraphs, a civil service that aimed to be free from political interference, a common-law, and an adversarial legal system.[^cite_note-129] This coincided with major changes in the world economy – industrialisation, and significant growth in production and trade. However, at the end of colonial rule, India inherited an economy that was one of the poorest in the developing world,[^cite_note-roy-2-130] with industrial development stalled, agriculture unable to feed a rapidly growing population, a largely illiterate and unskilled labour force, and extremely inadequate infrastructure.[^cite_note-AtIndependence-131]

The 1872 census revealed that 91.3% of the population of the region constituting present-day India resided in villages.[^cite_note-non-urban-132] This was a decline from the earlier Mughal era, when 85% of the population resided in villages and 15% in urban centres under [Akbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar)'s reign in 1600.[^cite_note-133] Urbanisation generally remained sluggish in British India until the 1920s, due to the lack of industrialisation and absence of adequate transportation. Subsequently, the policy of discriminating protection (where certain important industries were given financial protection by the state), coupled with the Second World War, saw the development and dispersal of industries, encouraging rural-urban migration, and in particular, the large port cities of [Bombay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombay), [Calcutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcutta) and [Madras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madras) grew rapidly. Despite this, only one-sixth of India's population lived in cities by 1951.[^cite_note-134]

The effect of British rule on India's economy is a controversial topic. Leaders of the [Indian independence movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_independence_movement) and [economic historians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history) have blamed colonial rule for India's poor economic performance following independence and argued that the capital required for the Industrial Revolution in Britain came from India. At the same time, other historians have countered that India's poor economic performance was due to various sectors being in a state of growth and decline due to changes brought in by colonialism and a world that was moving towards industrialisation and [economic integration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_integration).[^cite_note-roy-1-135]

Several economic historians have argued that Indian [real wages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_wage) declined in the early 19th century, or possibly beginning in the very late 18th century, largely as a result of British colonial rule. According to Prasannan Parthasarathi and Sashi Sivramkrishna, the grain wages of Indian weavers were likely comparable to that of their British counterparts and their average income was around five times the subsistence level, which was comparable to advanced parts of Europe.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-AL-137] However they concluded that due to the scarcity of data, it was hard to draw definitive conclusions and that more research was required.[^cite_note-WE-100][^cite_note-AL-137] It has also been argued that India went through a period of deindustrialization in the latter half of the 18th century as an indirect outcome of the collapse of the Mughal Empire.[^cite_note-williamson-99]

### Pre-liberalisation period (1947–1980)

Main articles: [Five-Year Plans of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five-Year_Plans_of_India), [Economic policy of the Indira Gandhi government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_Indira_Gandhi_government), and [Licence Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Licence_Raj)

Indian [economic policy](210522.md) after independence was influenced by the impact of the colonial experience, which was seen as exploitative by Indian leaders.[^cite_note-AtIndependence-131] Domestic policy tended towards protectionism, with a strong emphasis on [import substitution industrialisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Import_substitution_industrialisation), [economic interventionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_interventionism), a large government-run [public sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_sector), [business regulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulation_\(socio-legal_concept\)), and [central planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_planning),[^cite_note-138] while trade and foreign investment policies were relatively liberal.[^cite_note-139] [Five-Year Plans of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five-Year_Plans_of_India) resembled central planning in the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union). Steel, mining, machine tools, telecommunications, insurance, and power plants, among other industries, were effectively nationalised in the mid-1950s.[^cite_note-staley-140] The Indian economy of this period is characterised as [Dirigism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirigisme).[^cite_note-PE-77][^cite_note-BB-78]



Change in per capita GDP of India, 1820–2015. Figures are inflation-adjusted to 1990 International Geary-Khamis dollars.

[^cite_note-141]

[^cite_note-142]

> &#x20;Never talk to me about profit, Jeh, it is a dirty word.

— Nehru, India's Fabian Socialism-inspired first prime minister to industrialist [J. R. D. Tata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._R._D._Tata), when Tata suggested state-owned companies should be profitable[^cite_note-gdas2002-143]

[Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru), the first [prime minister of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_minister_of_India), along with the statistician [Prasanta Chandra Mahalanobis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prasanta_Chandra_Mahalanobis), formulated and oversaw economic policy during the initial years of the country's independence. They expected favourable outcomes from their strategy, involving the rapid development of [heavy industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_industry) by both public and [private sectors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_sector), and based on direct and indirect state intervention, rather than the more extreme [Soviet-style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Soviet_Union) central command system.[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-Cameron-1-145] The Avadi Resolution, in January 1955, declared that the objective of the Indian National Congress was the establishment of a "socialistic pattern of society" in India. The policy of concentrating simultaneously on capital- and technology-intensive heavy industry and subsidising manual, low-skill [cottage industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cottage_industries) was criticised by economist [Milton Friedman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milton_Friedman), who thought it would waste capital and labour, and retard the development of small manufacturers.[^cite_note-milton-1-146] A more sustained domestic criticism came from the economist [BR Shenoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellikoth_Raghunath_Shenoy), a former student of [Frederich Hayek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Hayek) at the [London School of Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_School_of_Economics). In 1955, Shenoy was the sole dissenter among the 21-member Panel of Economists convened to review the [Second Five Year Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five-Year_Plans_of_India), issuing a "Note of Dissent" that argued the plan's reliance on deficit financing and large-scale state-led industrialisation would generate chronic inflation and balance of payment pressure rather than sustained growth.[^cite_note-147] Consumer prices rose by roughly 30% over the course of the Plan, broadly as predicted by Shenoy.[^cite_note-\:5-148] It is considered the first systemic critique of economic planning in India, and Shenoy is often cited in retrospect as anticipating the balance-of-payments crisis in 1991.[^cite_note-\:5-148]

Domestic political opposition to economic planning came from the [Swatantra Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swatantra_Party), founded in 1959 by [C Rajagopalachari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._Rajagopalachari), a former Governor-General of India, together with [Minoo Masani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minoo_Masani) and [NG Ranga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N._G._Ranga). The party was formed partly in reaction to the Avadi Resolution in 1955 in which [Congress](149333.md) resolved to aim at a "socialistic pattern of society".[^cite_note-149] Swatantra advocated a [market-based economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy), dismantaling of the "[Licence Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Licence_Raj)", free trade, and protection of private property rights. It became the single largest opposition party, with 44 seats, following the [1967 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Indian_general_election), before its decline after Rajaji's death in 1972, and dissolving in 1974.[^cite_note-150]

> &#x20;I cannot decide how much to borrow, what shares to issue, at what price, what wages and bonus to pay, and what dividend to give. I even need the government's permission for the salary I pay to a senior executive.

— [J. R. D. Tata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._R._D._Tata), on the Indian regulatory system, 1969[^cite_note-gdas2002-143]

Since 1965, the use of [high-yielding varieties of seeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_seed), increased [fertilisers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertilisers) and improved [irrigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrigation) facilities collectively contributed to the [Green Revolution in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Revolution_in_India), which improved the condition of agriculture by increasing crop productivity, improving crop patterns and strengthening forward and backward linkages between agriculture and industry.[^cite_note-151] However, it has also been criticised as an unsustainable effort, resulting in the growth of capitalistic farming, ignoring institutional reforms and widening income disparities.[^cite_note-152]

### Economic reforms during the 1980s



Shatabdi Express predecessor to all modern trains launched in 1980's

Indira Gandhi took several liberalization steps after the coming back to power following the [1980 Indian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Indian_general_election). There were several steps taken such as opening of automobile sectors to private sector i.e. [Maruti Suzuki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maruti_Suzuki),[^cite_note-153] creation of auto component industries through new industrial zones known as [Industrial Model Townships(IMT)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manesar) and [Gurgaon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurgaon), expansion of [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel), [fertilizer](37401.md), [oil](4207510.md) and [cement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cement) sector led to wider participation of private sector.[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155] They also subsequently tried to do urban reforms such as creation of [Navi Mumbai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navi_Mumbai) and [Noida](986046.md).

In 1984, The *New Computer Policy of 1984* was introduced by [Rajiv Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajiv_Gandhi), as it eased import restrictions on technology, encouraged private investments, and provided incentives for software exports. This included computers, airlines, defense, and telecommunications. Gandhi's government also established Software Technology Parks (STPs) to provide infrastructure, tax benefits, and faster data communications, enabling companies to export software services globally.[^cite_note-156] Rajiv Gandhi's administration also saw setup of new logistics, telecom and transport infrastructure like [Nhava Sheva port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru_Port), [Centre for Development of Advanced Computing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_for_Development_of_Advanced_Computing), [Centre for Development of Telematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_for_Development_of_Telematics), [CONCOR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Container_Corporation_of_India), [IL\&FS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrastructure_Leasing_&_Financial_Services), [CAMS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_Age_Management_Services) and creation of highway body [NHAI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Highways_Authority_of_India).[^cite_note-157] These reforms led to the growth rate of 5.6% throughout 1980's instead of 2.9% in 1970's but [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) was the largest export partner to India, which would lead to 1991 crisis.[^cite_note-158]

In 1990 [Rajiv Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajiv_Gandhi) introduced measures to significantly reduce the [Licence Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Licence_Raj) such as allowing private businesses and individuals to purchase capital, consumer goods and import without bureaucratic restrictions.[^cite_note-\:1-159] Gandhi subsequently promised full economic liberalization and made [V. P. Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V._P._Singh) the finance minister, who tried to reduce tax evasion and tax receipts rose due to this crackdown although taxes were lowered.[^cite_note-\:1-159] This process lost its momentum during the later tenure of Mr. Gandhi as his government was marred by scandals.[^cite_note-\:1-159]

### Post-liberalisation period (since 1991-2021)

Main articles: [Economic history of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_India), [Economic liberalisation in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalisation_in_India), [Economic development in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_development_in_India), and [Economic policy of the Narendra Modi government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_Narendra_Modi_government)

See also: [Viksit Bharat @2047](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viksit_Bharat_@2047)

P. V. Narasimha Rao

[P. V. Narasimha Rao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._V._Narasimha_Rao)

Manmohan Singh

[Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh)

[Economic liberalisation in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalisation_in_India) was initiated in 1991 by Prime Minister P. V. Narasimha Rao and his then-Finance Minister Dr. Manmohan Singh.

The collapse of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), which was India's major trading partner, and the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War), which caused a spike in oil prices, resulted in a major balance-of-payments crisis for India, which found itself facing the prospect of defaulting on its loans.[^cite_note-Ghosh-160] India asked for a \$1.8 billion bailout loan from the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) (IMF), which in return demanded de-regulation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETask_Force_Report20067-161]

In response, the [Narasimha Rao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._V._Narasimha_Rao) government, including Finance Minister [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh), initiated [economic reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalisation_in_India) in 1991. The reforms did away with the [Licence Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Licence_Raj), reduced tariffs and interest rates and ended many public monopolies, allowing automatic approval of [foreign direct investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment) in many sectors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETask_Force_Report20067–8-162] Since then, the overall thrust of liberalisation has remained the same, although no government has tried to take on powerful lobbies such as trade unions and farmers, on contentious issues such as reforming labour laws and reducing [agricultural subsidies](171866.md).[^cite_note-Gandhi-1-163] This has been accompanied by increases in life expectancy, literacy rates, and food security, although urban residents have benefited more than rural residents.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETask_Force_Report200617–20-164]



GDP grows exponentially, almost doubling every 8-9 years.



Indian GDP growth rate from 1985 to 2024, compared to that of China

From 2010, India has risen from ninth-largest to the fifth-largest economies in the world by nominal GDP in 2019 by surpassing UK, [France](5843419.md), [Italy](14532.md) and [Brazil](3383.md).[^cite_note-165]

India started recovery in 2013–14 when the GDP growth rate accelerated to 6.4% from the previous year's 5.5%. The acceleration continued through 2014–15 and 2015–16 with growth rates of 7.5% and 8.0% respectively. India grew faster than China which registered 6.9% growth in 2015. However the growth rate subsequently decelerated, to 7.1% and 6.6% in 2016–17 and 2017–18 respectively,[^cite_note-166] partly because of the disruptive effects of [2016 Indian banknote demonetisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Indian_banknote_demonetisation) and the [Goods and Services Tax (India)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goods_and_Services_Tax_\(India\)).[^cite_note-167] But after slowdown due to global disruption caused by [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) in 2019, India's economic growth has consistently outperformed China every year by a large margin and stays among fastest growing major economy in the world according to [World Bank](36752.md).[^cite_note-168]

### COVID-19 pandemic and new reforms (2021–present)

Main article: [Economic impact of the COVID-19 pandemic in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_India)

During the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_India), numerous [rating agencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rating_agencies) downgraded India's GDP predictions for FY21 to negative figures,[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170] signalling a recession in India, the most severe since 1979.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172] The Indian Economy contracted by 6.6 percent which was lower than the estimated 7.3 percent decline.[^cite_note-173] In 2022, the ratings agency [Fitch Ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitch_Ratings) upgraded India's outlook to stable similar to [S\&P Global Ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S\&P_Global_Ratings) and [Moody's Investors Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moody's_Investors_Service)'s outlooks.[^cite_note-174] In the first quarter of financial year 2022–2023, the Indian economy grew by 13.5%.[^cite_note-175]

With 607 million workers, India has the [world's second-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_labour_force) labour force, which is growing rapidly, with 46.6 million added in 2023–24 alone.[^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177] India's fertility rate continues to fall sharply, from about 3.3 in 2000 to around 1.9 in 2025, below the replacement level of 2.1.[^cite_note-178] [Labour productivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workforce_productivity) remains lower than in advanced economies but is similar to levels observed in emerging Asian countries such as China.[^cite_note-179] India's [outsourcing industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outsourcing) has shifted from traditional call centre [BPO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BPO_Industry) work, now largely handled by the Philippines and other Southeast Asian countries, to high-value software development, [consultancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consultant), engineering, and research oriented services.[^cite_note-180] India hosts more than half of the world's [Global Capability Centres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_capability_center) (GCCs), which perform product engineering and R\&D functions.[^cite_note-181] It is home to about 20% of global [semiconductor chip design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Processor_design) engineers and, as of early 2025, has an estimated 4.3 million software engineers, accounting for roughly 14.7% of the global software engineering workforce.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183]

During the 2020s, India established [free trade agreements](2533562.md) and economic‑partnership with several countries and regional blocs, including the [European Union](9317.md), [ASEAN](106539.md), [SAFTA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Free_Trade_Area), alongside many other with world nations.[^cite_note-184] In April 2025, the Indian economy temporarily surpassed [Japan](15573.md) to become the [fourth-largest economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) in the world in terms of GDP (nominal).[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186] In November 2025, the IMF gave the Indian economy a letter 'C' grade, mentioning that Indian [national accounts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_accounts) and inflation data is not accurately recording the informal sector and people's spending patterns.[^cite_note-d952-72]

### 2026

In January 2026, India and the EU have announced they have agreed on what has become known as the "mother of all deals."[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188] After 20 years of negotiations they have reached an agreement on a long term [free trade agreement](2533562.md) (FTA). The final signing will take place once all legal issues are resolved. Both sides hope it will be effective in early 2027.[^cite_note-\:2-189][^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191]

In the first quarter of FY 2026–27, India's economy grew by 7.8%, exceeding market estimates by about 50 basis points. Following the release of the data, economists raised their full-year GDP growth projections to around 7%. In its August 2026 *Monthly Economic Review*, the [Ministry of Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Finance_\(India\)) stated that domestic economic activity, inflation and the external position had remained relatively stable despite global economic uncertainty, supported by resilient domestic demand, easing cost pressures and comfortable foreign-exchange reserves.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193]
