## Regulatory environment

Main articles: [Regulatory burden in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulatory_burden_in_India) and [Business environment in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_environment_in_India)

Even as the License Raj was formally dismantled in 1991, India's economy continues to operate under an excessive compliance regime. A 2022 report by the Observer Research Foundation and TeamLease Services noted that Indian businesses are burdened with over 69,000 individual compliance obligations and 6,600 recurring statutory filings each year under 1536 federal and state laws.[^cite_note-\:4-200] India ranked 86th out of 165 economies in [Fraser Institute's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraser_Institute) [Economic Freedom of the World Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Freedom_of_the_World), scoring 6.58 out of 10.[^cite_note-201] Economists have linked persistent compliance burden and business environment in India for the country having \~90% of its workforce being employed in the informal sector.[^cite_note-202]

## Tariffs and industrial policy

Main article: [Foreign trade of India](33475686.md)

See also: [Atmanirbhar Bharat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmanirbhar_Bharat) and [Production Linked Incentive schemes in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Production_Linked_Incentive_schemes_in_India)

For two decades, India's import duties fell. However, since 2018, India's trade regime has become more protectionist.[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204] The government has increased tariffs on electronics, automobile components, textiles, and a range of consumer goods according to Cato Institute, a free-market think tank.[^cite_note-205] The [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization)'s 2021 review of India's trade policy found that the simple average [most favoured nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Most_favoured_nation) rate rose from 13% in 2014-15 to 14.3% in 2020-21.[^cite_note-\:6-206] The review also described India as WTO's main user of [anti-dumping measures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dumping_\(pricing_policy\)), with 233 investigations initiated between 2015 and 2019 and 254 anti-dumping duties in force at the end of 2019.[^cite_note-\:6-206]

In May 2020, the government announced "[Atmanirbhar Bharat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmanirbhar_Bharat)" (self-reliant India), a package of stimulus and Industrial Policy measures presented as reducing dependence on imports.[^cite_note-207] The [Production Linked Incentive Schemes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Production_Linked_Incentive_schemes_in_India), announced as part of the package with an initial budget outlay of ₹40,000 crore (equivalent to ₹510 billion or US\$5.3 billion in 2026), expanded to a budget outlay of ₹1.97 lakh crore (US\$20 billion) across 14 sectors by 2026.[^cite_note-208]

## Economic sectors

Percent of share in GDP of India contributing from various sector in 2023-24

1. [Financial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_services), [real estate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_estate) & [professional services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional_services) (22.9%)

2. [Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_in_services), [hotels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotels), [transport](14602.md), [communication](14601.md) and [broadcasting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadcasting) (18.3%)

3. [Manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manufacturing) (17.0%)

4. [Agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_India), [forestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forestry) and [fishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishing) (14.3%)

5. [Public Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Administration), [defence](608296.md) and other services (12.5%)

6. [Construction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Construction) (8.89%)

7. [Electricity](9550.md), [gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas), [water supply](684928.md) & other utility services (2.32%)

8. [Mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining) & [quarrying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quarrying) (2.09%)

### Agriculture, fishing, and forestry

Main articles: [Agriculture in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_India), [Animal husbandry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_husbandry_in_India), [Fishing in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishing_in_India), and [Forestry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forestry_in_India)

Agriculture and allied sectors like forestry, logging and fishing accounted for 18.4% of the GDP,[^cite_note-SI-9] the sector employed 51.2 crore persons or 45.5% of the workforce in India are employed in agriculture.[^cite_note-agr_emp-209][^cite_note-210] India is major agriculture producing country and has the most arable land in the world followed by the United States.[^cite_note-211] However, agricultural output lags far behind its potential.[^cite_note-nytagriculture-212] Agriculture's contribution to GDP has steadily declined from 1951 to 2023, shifting from 52% to 15% of India's GDP[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214] yet it is still the country's largest employment provider sector .[^cite_note-agr_emp-209] Crop-yield-per-unit-area of all crops has grown since 1950, due to the special emphasis placed on agriculture in the five-year plans and steady improvements in irrigation, technology, application of modern agricultural practices and provision of agricultural credit and subsidies since the Green Revolution in India. International comparisons reveal the average yield in India is generally 30% to 50% of the highest average yield in the world.[^cite_note-Datt-5-215] The states of [Uttar Pradesh](231623.md), [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab,_India), Haryana, [Madhya Pradesh](47945.md), Andhra Pradesh, [Telangana](990267.md), Bihar, [West Bengal](34040.md), Gujarat and [Maharashtra](20629.md) are key contributors to Indian agriculture.

India receives an average annual rainfall of 1,208 millimetres (47.6 in) and a total annual [precipitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precipitation_\(meteorology\)) of 4,000 billion cubic metres, with the total utilisable [water resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_resources_in_India), including surface and [groundwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groundwater), amounting to 1,123 billion cubic metres.[^cite_note-216] 546,820 square kilometres (211,130 mi<sup>2</sup>) of the land area, or about 39% of the total cultivated area, is irrigated.[^cite_note-irrigation-217] India's inland water resources and marine resources provide employment to nearly 6 million people in the fisheries sector. In 2023, according to the Ministry of Fisheries, India is the 3rd largest fish producing and 2nd largest aquaculture producing nation in the world.



India exports more than 100,000 tonnes (98,000 long tons; 110,000 short tons) of processed cashew kernels every year. There are more than 600 cashew processing units in [Kollam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kollam) alone.

[^cite_note-CI-218]

India is the largest producer of milk, jute and [pulses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legume), and has the world's largest cattle population with 303 million animals in 2023.[^cite_note-219] It is the second-largest producer of rice, wheat, sugarcane, cotton and [groundnuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peanut), as well as the second-largest fruit and vegetable producer, accounting for 10.9% and 8.6% of the world fruit and vegetable production, respectively, but only for 1% of global fruits and vegetables trade. India is also the second-largest producer and the largest consumer of silk, producing 77,000 tonnes (76,000 long tons; 85,000 short tons) in 2005.[^cite_note-fao-silk-220] India is the second-largest exporter of cashew kernels and cashew nut shell liquid (CNSL). Foreign exchange earned by the country through the export of cashew kernels during FY 2023 reached 356M\$. 76,624 tonnes (75,414 long tons; 84,464 short tons) of kernels were exported during 2023.[^cite_note-221] There are about 600 cashew processing units in [Kollam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kollam), Kerala.[^cite_note-CI-218]

India's foodgrain production stagnant at approximately 316 megatonnes (311 million long tons; 348 million short tons) during 2020–21.[^cite_note-222] India exports several agriculture products, such as Basmati rice, wheat, cereals, spices, fresh fruits, dry fruits, cotton, tea, coffee, milk products and other cash crops to the Asian, African and other countries.[^cite_note-223]



Sugarcane weighing at a [Pravara Sahakari Sakhar Karkhana Ltd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperative_sugar_factories_in_Maharashtra) in Maharashtra

### Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSME)

See also: [Ministry of Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Micro,_Small_and_Medium_Enterprises)

India began its first few steps during the years 1978-80 when early conditions for SMEs or entrepreneurship were hostile too. 63 million MSMEs in India which contribute *35% to the country's GDP* provides employment to 111.4 million persons and accounts for more than 40% of India's exports and are hailed as the 'growth engines' of the economy. China has been creating 16,000-18,000 new enterprises per day for the last 5 years. When one compares that with India, it is about 1000-1100 per day.[^cite_note-224]

### Industrial machinery, tools and manufacturing equipment

See also: [List of vehicle plants in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_vehicle_plants_in_India) and [Tractors in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tractors_in_India)

Machinery and equipment market is expected to grow 8% from 2024 to 2029. India's Industrial Machinery Equipment and Tools market size is expected to be \$210 billion in 2023.The rise in R\&D and large number of startups has led to increase in investment in tools, industrial equipment, robotics, industrial automation, pharmaceutical machinery, mining & construction equipment.[^cite_note-225] The [Indian Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Government) has launched an initiative in promoting electrification of fossil-fuel based equipment hence reducing carbon footprint and leading to new innovations.[^cite_note-226]

### Pulp and paper industry

Main article: [Pulp and paper industry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulp_and_paper_industry_in_India)

This section is an excerpt from [Pulp and paper industry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulp_and_paper_industry_in_India).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Pulp_and_paper_industry_in_India\&action=edit)]



Ancient Sanskrit on hemp based paper. Hemp fiber was commonly used in the production of paper from 200 BCE to the late 1800s.

The [pulp and paper industry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulp_and_paper_industry_in_India) is the 5th largest industrial sector in India.[^cite_note-227] India accounts for approximately 5% of global paper production and ranks 15th globally in terms of total output.[^cite_note-Pulp_and_paper_industry_in_India_brick-228] The industry provides 0.5 million direct and 1.5 million indirect employment. Its estimated turnover was ₹80,000 crore in 2024.[^cite_note-229] There are 900 paper mills in India, with most of them being located in [Gujarat](53707.md), [Maharashtra](20629.md) and [Uttar Pradesh](231623.md).[^cite_note-Pulp_and_paper_industry_in_India_uja-230]

It produces a wide range of products including writing and printing paper, newsprint, packaging board and specialty paper. The sector plays a key role in supporting education, information circulation and packaging.[^cite_note-Pulp_and_paper_industry_in_India_ibef-231] Production capacity is around 25–26 million tonnes annually, with installed capacity at about 30-32 million tonnes. Per capita paper consumption is relatively low: about 15–17 kg per year.[^cite_note-232]

### Mining, resources, and chemicals

#### Mining

Main article: [Mining in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_in_India)



an [NLC India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NLC_India_Limited) mine

India has, in 2022, a reported 1,319 mines of which reporting mines for metallic minerals were estimated at 545 and non-metallic minerals at 775.[^cite_note-233]

Mining contributed to 1.75% of GDP and employed directly or indirectly 11 million people in 2021.[^cite_note-234] India's mining industry was the fourth-largest producer of minerals in the world by volume, and eighth-largest producer by value in 2009.[^cite_note-mospimines-235] In 2013, it mined and processed 89 minerals, of which four were fuel, three were atomic energy minerals, and 80 non-fuel.[^cite_note-236] The public sector accounted for 68% of mineral production by volume in 2011–12.[^cite_note-ficcimine-237] India has the world's fourth-largest [natural resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_resources_of_India), with the [mining sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_in_India) contributing 11% of the country's industrial GDP and 2.5% of total GDP.

Nearly 50% of India's mining industry, by output value, is concentrated in eight states: [Odisha](250724.md), Rajasthan, [Chhattisgarh](47734.md), Andhra Pradesh, [Telangana](990267.md), Jharkhand, [Madhya Pradesh](47945.md) and [Karnataka](16880.md). Another 25% of the output by value comes from offshore oil and gas resources.[^cite_note-ficcimine-237] India operated about 3,000 mines in 2010, half of which were coal, limestone and iron ore.[^cite_note-238] On output-value basis, India was one of the five largest producers of mica, chromite, coal, lignite, iron ore, bauxite, barite, zinc and manganese; while being one of the ten largest global producers of many other minerals.[^cite_note-mospimines-235][^cite_note-ficcimine-237] India was the fourth-largest producer of steel in 2013,[^cite_note-239] and the seventh-largest producer of aluminium.[^cite_note-240]

India also holds the world's third-largest reserves of [rare earth elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rare_earth_elements) (approx. 6.9 million tonnes; 7% of global reserves), with deposits concentrated in states such as Odisha, Andhra Pradesh, Tamil Nadu, Kerala and West Bengal. Despite its large reserves, its high-value processing capability is limited and rely heavily on imports from China.[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242]

#### Iron and steel

Main article: [Iron and steel industry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_and_steel_industry_in_India)

See also: [History of metallurgy in the Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_metallurgy_in_the_Indian_subcontinent)



[Bokaro Steel Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bokaro_Steel_Plant) (BSL) alone contributes 45% of [SAIL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_Authority_of_India)'s profit and it produces highly diversified steel portfolio

India surpassed Japan as the second largest steel producer in January 2019.[^cite_note-243] As per worldsteel, India's crude steel production in 2018 was at 106.5 tonnes (104.8 long tons; 117.4 short tons), 4.9% increase from 101.5 tonnes (99.9 long tons; 111.9 short tons) in 2017, which means that India overtook Japan as the world's second largest steel production country.

#### Petroleum

Main article: [Oil and gas industry in India](52315795.md)



[MRPL Refinery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangalore_Refinery_and_Petrochemicals_Limited) in [Mangalore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangalore)

Petroleum products and chemicals are a major contributor to India's industrial GDP, and together they contribute over 34% of its export earnings. India hosts [many oil refinery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_oil_refineries_in_India) and petrochemical operations developed with help of [Soviet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) technology such as [Barauni Refinery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barauni_Refinery) and [Gujarat Refinery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujarat_Refinery), it also includes the world's largest refinery complex in [Jamnagar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamnagar) that processes 1.24 million barrels of crude per day.[^cite_note-244] By volume, the Indian chemical industry was the third-largest producer in Asia, and contributed 5% of the country's GDP. India is one of the five-largest producers of agrochemicals, polymers and plastics, dyes and various organic and inorganic chemicals.[^cite_note-245] Despite being a large producer and exporter, India is a net importer of chemicals due to domestic demands.[^cite_note-246] India's [chemical industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_industry_in_India) is extremely diversified and estimated at \$178 billion.[^cite_note-247]

#### Chemicals and fertilizer

Main article: [Chemical industry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_industry_in_India)

The chemical industry contributed \$163 billion to the economy in FY18 and is expected to reach \$300–400 billion by 2025.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249] The industry employed 17.33 million people (4% of the workforce) in 2016.[^cite_note-WTTCBenchmark-250]

### Textile

Main article: [Textile industry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_industry_in_India)

This section is an excerpt from [Textile industry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_industry_in_India).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Textile_industry_in_India\&action=edit)]

The [textile industry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_industry_in_India), traditionally after [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_India), is the only industry in the country that has generated large-scale employment for both skilled and unskilled labour. The [textile industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_industry) continues to be the second-largest employment generating sector in [India](14533.md). It offers direct employment to over 35 million people in the country.[^cite_note-Textile_industry_in_India_cci-251] India is the world's second largest exporter of [textiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile) and [clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clothing_industry), and in the [fiscal year](5752099.md) 2022, the exports stood at US\$44.4 billion.[^cite_note-252] According to the [Ministry of Textiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Textiles), the share of textiles in total exports during April–July 2010 was 11.04%. During 2009–2010, the Indian textile industry was pegged at US\$55 billion, 64% of which services domestic demand.[^cite_note-Textile_industry_in_India_cci-251] In 2010, there were 2,500 textile weaving factories and 4,135 textile finishing factories in all of India.[^cite_note-Textile_industry_in_India_ari-253] According to [AT Kearney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AT_Kearney)’s ‘Retail Apparel Index’, India was ranked as the fourth most promising market for apparel retailers in 2009.[^cite_note-254]

India is the second largest producer of fibre. The country is the world's largest producer of [cotton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton) and [jute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jute).[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256] India is also the world's second largest producer of [silk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk).[^cite_note-257] Other fibres produced in India include [wool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wool), and [man-made fibres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man-made_fibers). 100% [FDI is allowed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FDI_in_India) via automatic route in textile sector. [Rieter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rieter), Trutzschler, [Saurer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saurer), Soktas, Zambiati, Bilsar, Monti, CMT, [E-land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E-land), [Nisshinbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nisshinbo_Holdings), [Marks & Spencer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marks_&_Spencer), [Zara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zara_\(retailer\)), [Promod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promod), [Benetton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benetton_Group), and [Levi’s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levi%E2%80%99s) are some of the foreign textile companies invested or working in India.[^cite_note-258] Between January and July 2021, India exported textile products worth Rs 1.77 lakh crore, which is 52.6% more than the same period last year.[^cite_note-259]

### Pharmaceuticals

Main article: [Pharmaceutical industry in India](3373039.md)

This section is an excerpt from [Pharmaceutical industry in India](3373039.md).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Pharmaceutical_industry_in_India\&action=edit)]

The [pharmaceutical industry in India](3373039.md) was valued at an estimated US\$50 billion in FY 2023-24 and is estimated to reach \$130 billion by 2030.[^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261] India is often described as the “pharmacy of the world,” supplying around one-fifth of global demand for generic medicines and exporting pharmaceuticals to over 200 countries in 2023–24, with approximately 70% of exports destined for highly regulated markets such as North America and Europe.[^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263] In the United States, Indian pharmaceutical companies supply nearly half of all generic prescriptions by volume.[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265] India is also the largest vaccine manufacturer globally by volume, accounting for over 60% of the world’s vaccine production.[^cite_note-266]

According to [Economic Survey 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Union_budget_of_India), the turnover in the domestic pharmaceutical market was estimated to be \$41 billion.[^cite_note-267] India's pharmaceutical exports revenue was \$25.3 billion in fiscal year 2022–23, according to the data released by [Pharmexcil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutical_Export_Promotion_Council).[^cite_note-268] India ranked third globally in terms of dollar value of drugs and medicines exports.[^cite_note-Pharmaceutical_industry_in_India_\:8-269]

Major pharmaceutical hubs in India are, broadly anticlockwise from the northwest: [Baddi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baddi), [Paonta Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paonta_Sahib), [Sikkim](140372.md), [Kolkata](47905.md), [Visakhapatnam](238760.md), [Hyderabad](37534.md), [Bangalore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangalore), [Mysore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mysore), [Chennai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chennai), [Pondicherry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pondicherry), [Goa](40010153.md), [Mumbai](19189.md), [Navi Mumbai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navi_Mumbai), [Pune](164634.md), [Aurangabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurangabad,_Maharashtra), [Indore](365670.md), [Pithampur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pithampur), [Ahmedabad](206337.md), [Vadodara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vadodara), [Ankleshwar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ankleshwar), and [Vapi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vapi).

### Transportation

Main article: [Transport in India](14602.md)

#### Railways and Logistics

See also: [List of railway lines in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_railway_lines_in_India)



&#x20;[Vande Bharat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vande_Bharat_\(trainset\)) train

The [Indian Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Railways) contributes to \~3% of the country's gross domestic product (GDP) and has social obligations pegged at \$5.3 billion annually.[^cite_note-270] Indian Railways revenue has grown at 5% CAGR in the past 5 years but profitability has reduced drastically in the past 4 years, due to growing infrastructure and modernization expenses. With a workforce of 1.31 million people, the IR is also one of the country's largest employers. The railways is a major contributor to jobs, GDP, and mobility.[^cite_note-271]

Indian Railways has decided to revise its 2022–23 rolling stock production plan upwards. The Ministry's new plan targets the production of 8,429 units for the coming financial year. Production for 2022–23 has been raised by 878 units from the earlier planned 7,551, according to the revised targets.[^cite_note-272] Indian Railways has targeted to manufacture 475 new [Vande Bharat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vande_Bharat_\(trainset\)) trainsets for the next four years as a part of its modernization plan.[^cite_note-273] It is about Rs 40,000 crore(\$5 billion) business opportunity that would also create 15,000 jobs and several spin-off benefits.[^cite_note-274] [Indian Railway's CORE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Organisation_for_Railway_Electrification) aims to electrify all of its broad gauge network by 31 March 2024.[^cite_note-275] The entire electrified mainline rail network in India uses [25 kV AC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/25_kV_AC_railway_electrification); DC is used only for [metros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_rail_transit_in_India). As of July 2023, India currently has 90% of total train tracks fully electrified.[^cite_note-276]

#### Rapid Transit

Main article: [Urban rail transit in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_rail_transit_in_India)



As of 2024, India has the [third largest metro system in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metro_systems#List_of_countries_by_system_length)

India is developing modern [mass rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_rapid_transit) systems to meet present and future urban requirements. A modern metro rail system is already in place in the cities of [Navi Mumbai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navi_Mumbai_Metro), [Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Metro), [Mumbai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mumbai_Metro), [Bangalore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangalore_Metro), [Kolkata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolkata_Metro), [Hyderabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyderabad_Metro), [Kochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kochi_Metro), [Gurgaon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_Metro_Gurgaon), [Jaipur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaipur_Metro), [Noida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noida_Metro), [Pune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pune_Metro), [Nagpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagpur_Metro), [Kanpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanpur_Metro), [Ahmedabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmedabad_Metro) and [Lucknow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucknow_Metro). Similar mass transit systems are intended for [Agra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agra), [Bhopal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhopal), [Indore](365670.md), [Surat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surat), [Patna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patna), [Bhubaneswar Tri-city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhubaneswar), [Chandigarh Tri-city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandigarh), Gwalior, [Mysore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mysore), [Nashik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashik_Metro), [Prayagraj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allahabad_Metro), [Varanasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varanasi_Metro), [Ranchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranchi), [Thane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thane) and [Trivandrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trivandrum). Former Prime Minister [Atal Bihari Vajpayee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atal_Bihari_Vajpayee) has been credited with success of the metro systems in India and every metro has followed [Delhi Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Metro) model generating lot of real estate wealth in India specially in smaller cities like [Gurgaon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurgaon) and [Noida](986046.md). For Elevated corridor, there is no need for land acquisition as pillars are built above [Median strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_strip) of a road.[^cite_note-277] Land prices in tier-II cities such as Lucknow, Patna, Jaipur, Ahmedabad, Pune, Kochi, and Coimbatore have gone up by almost 8-10 percent following the introduction of a metro corridor in these cities, an assessment by JLL has said.[^cite_note-278]

#### Aviation

Main article: [Civil aviation in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_aviation_in_India)

See also: [List of airlines of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airlines_of_India)

India is the fourth-largest civil aviation market in the world recording an air traffic of 158 million passengers in 2017.[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-IATA-280] The market is estimated to have 800 aircraft by 2020, which would account for 4.3% of global volumes,[^cite_note-281] and is expected to record annual passenger traffic of 520 million by 2037.[^cite_note-IATA-280] [IATA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IATA) estimated that aviation contributed \$30 billion to India's GDP in 2017, and supported 7.5 million jobs – 390,000 directly, 570,000 in the value chain, and 6.2 million through tourism.[^cite_note-IATA-280]

As of 2024, There are 75 new airports have been built in the last ten years, taking the total count to 149 airports (include helipads and aerodromes).[^cite_note-282]

#### Shipbuilding

Main article: [List of Indian shipbuilders and shipyards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indian_shipbuilders_and_shipyards)



*Maharishi Parshuram* built in 2002 by Cochin Shipyard for SCI

The Shipbuilding Financial Assistance Policy (SBFAP) was introduced in 2016, provides financial assistance to Indian shipyards for shipbuilding contracts. According to the ministry of ports, shipping and waterways, under SBFAP, a total of 313 domestic and export vessel orders were procured by 39 shipyards since the inception of the scheme, with the total value standing at about ₹10,500 crore(\$1.26 billion).[^cite_note-283]

India has multiple ship building companies such as [Cochin Shipyard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cochin_Shipyard), [Hindustan Shipyard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustan_Shipyard) and [Swan Defence and Heavy Industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swan_Defence_and_Heavy_Industries_Limited), mainly produces ships for European, South American and African shipping companies. Cochin shipyard is the pioneer in autonomous electric propulsion ships.[^cite_note-284]

### IT and telecommunications

Main article: [Information technology in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_technology_in_India)

#### Datacentre and cloud

Main article: [Data centre industry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_centre_industry_in_India)

India has emerged as the leading data centre hub in the Asia-Pacific region (excluding China), surpassing established players like Singapore, Australia, South Korea, Japan, and Hong Kong in installed capacity. This reflects the escalating demand for data services in one of the world's fastest-growing major economies. With a current installed capacity of 950 MW and projections indicating an additional 850 MW by 2026, India is poised to solidify its position as a key player in the Asia-Pacific data centre landscape.[^cite_note-285]

#### Telecommunications

Main article: [Telecommunications in India](14601.md)

See also: [List of telecom companies in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_telecom_companies_in_India) and [Internet in India](15500494.md)



[INSAT-1B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INSAT-1B) [satellite](27683.md): broadcasting sector in India is highly dependent on [INSAT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INSAT) system.

The telecommunication sector generated ₹2.20 trillion (US\$23 billion) in revenue in 2014–15, accounting for 1.94% of total GDP.[^cite_note-286] Telecom equipment manufacturing and exports are becoming a new success story following the success of smartphone exports. In the financial year 2024, the telecom equipment production exceeded the milestone of Rs 45,000 crore with exports totaling approximately Rs 10,500 crore.[^cite_note-287]

### Defence

Main article: [Defence industry of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_industry_of_India)

See also: [Space industry of India](66768914.md)



PAFC AIP fuel-cell module developed by the [DRDO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_Research_and_Development_Organisation) of [India](14533.md) to power marine transport

With over 1.3 million active personnel, the [Indian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Army) is the [third-largest military force and the largest volunteer army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_military_and_paramilitary_personnel). Defence expenditure was pegged at US\$70.12 billion for fiscal year 2022–23 and, increased 9.8% than previous fiscal year.[^cite_note-288] India is the world's second largest arms importer; between 2016 and 2020, it accounted for 9.5% of the total [global arms imports](608296.md).[^cite_note-289] India exported military hardware worth ₹159.2 billion (US\$1.7 billion) in the financial year 2022–23, the highest ever and a notable tenfold increase since 2016–17.[^cite_note-290]

### Energy

Main articles: [Energy in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_India), [Electricity sector in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_sector_in_India), and [Energy policy of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy_of_India)

See also: [Renewable energy in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_in_India)



An [IPHWR-700](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPHWR-700) reactor undergoing construction in [Kakrapar Nuclear Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakrapar_Atomic_Power_Station) started in 1984

[Primary energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_energy) consumption of India is the third-largest after China and US with 5.3% global share in the year 2015.[^cite_note-srwew-291] Coal and crude oil together account for 85% of the primary energy consumption of India. India's [oil reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_reserves) meet 25% of the country's domestic oil demand.[^cite_note-CIA-292][^cite_note-Datt-1-293] As of April 2015, India's total proven crude oil reserves are 763.476 megatonnes (751.418 million long tons; 841.588 million short tons), while gas reserves stood at 1,490 billion cubic metres (53 trillion cubic feet).[^cite_note-png-294] Oil and natural gas fields are located offshore at [Ashoknagar Oil Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashoknagar_Oil_Field), [Bombay High](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombay_High), [Krishna Godavari Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krishna_Godavari_Basin), [Mangala Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangala_Area) and the [Cauvery Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cauvery_Delta), and onshore mainly in the states of [West Bengal](34040.md), [Assam](186162.md), Gujarat and [Rajasthan](26291.md). India is the fourth-largest consumer of oil and net oil imports were nearly ₹8.2 trillion (US\$85 billion) in 2014–15,[^cite_note-png-294] which had an adverse effect on the country's [current account deficit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Current_account_deficit). The petroleum industry in India mostly consists of public sector companies such as [Oil and Natural Gas Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_and_Natural_Gas_Corporation) (ONGC), [Hindustan Petroleum Corporation Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustan_Petroleum_Corporation_Limited) (HPCL), [Bharat Petroleum Corporation Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharat_Petroleum_Corporation_Limited) (BPCL) and Indian Oil Corporation Limited (IOCL). There are some major private Indian companies in the oil sector such as [Reliance Industries Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reliance_Industries_Limited) (RIL) which operates the world's largest oil refining complex.[^cite_note-295] In 2026, fuel costs rose after the [Iran war](82533582.md) disrupted Middle Eastern energy supplies, leading to higher LPG prices in India.[^cite_note-296]

India became the world's third-largest producer of electricity in 2013 with a 4.8% global share in electricity generation, surpassing Japan and Russia.[^cite_note-srwe-297]

### Infrastructure

Main articles: [Roads in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roads_in_India), [Indian Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Railways), [List of airports in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_India), and [Ports in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ports_in_India)



[Vizhinjam International Seaport Thiruvananthapuram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vizhinjam_International_Seaport_Thiruvananthapuram), the first *Mother Port* in the world, with harbour depth of 24 metres

India's infrastructure and transport sector contributes around 5% to the country's gross domestic product (GDP).[^cite_note-298]

#### Roads

India's total [road network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Road_network) is over 6.6 million km, making it the [second largest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_road_network_size) as of early 2025. This expansive network comprises [national highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_highways_of_India), [state highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_highways_in_India), and other roads and is crucial for transportation, with roads carrying over 85% of passenger traffic and more than 70% of freight.[^cite_note-299]

India has rapidly expanded its network of expressways and national highways. As of 2024, more than 45,000 kilometres (28,000 mi) of 4-lane and above highways have been built, including flagship projects such as the [Delhi–Mumbai Expressway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi%E2%80%93Mumbai_Expressway), [Surat–Chennai Expressway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surat%E2%80%93Chennai_Expressway), [Ganga Expressway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganga_Expressway), [Dwarka Expressway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwarka_Expressway), and [Delhi–Meerut Expressway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi%E2%80%93Meerut_Expressway).[^cite_note-300]



[Delhi–Meerut Expressway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi%E2%80%93Meerut_Expressway) is among the widest expressways in India

#### Ports

India has a coastline of 11,098.81 kilometres (6,896.48 mi), with 13 major ports and more than 200 non-major and private ports.[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302] These handle around 95% of India's external trade by volume and about 70% by value. [Kandla (Deendayal) Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandla_Port) in Gujarat is the largest public port, while [Mundra Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mundra_Port) (operated by Adani Ports) is the largest private port in India. Under the [Sagarmala project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagarmala_project), port-led development and modernization of coastal infrastructure are being accelerated.

#### Airports

India currently has around 153 operational airports managed by the [Airport Authority of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airports_Authority_of_India) (AAI), including 29 [international airports](1519705.md), 10 [customs airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Customs_airport), and 114 [domestic airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_airport), along with 23 civil enclaves at defence [airfields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airfields). This number reflects significant growth, as it was 74 in 2014 and has more than doubled, a result of government initiatives [UDAN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UDAN).[^cite_note-303]

### Construction and Real Estate

Main articles: [Construction industry in India](11490279.md), [List of megaprojects in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_megaprojects_in_India), and [Housing in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_in_India)

The construction sector—including infrastructure and real estate services—now accounts for approximately 18% of India's total economic output, reflecting a decade-long compound annual growth rate (CAGR) of about 11%.[^cite_note-304] It remains the second-largest employment generator in the economy, currently employing roughly 71 million people and forecasted to exceed 100 million jobs by 2030.[^cite_note-305] India is massively privatizing [airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airports), ports, [bus stands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-State_Bus_Terminals), [railway stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railway_stations), [dams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dams), [dam wind park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_park), [solar parks](34779206.md), [floating solar plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Floating_solar), [power transmission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_transmission), [highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highways), [thermal power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermal_power) and other utilizes.[^cite_note-306]

### Finance and trade

#### Banking and financial services

Main articles: [Banking in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_in_India) and [Finance in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finance_in_India)

See also: [List of banks in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_banks_in_India), [Insurance in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurance_in_India), [Money market in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Money_market_in_India), and [Financial regulation in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_regulation_in_India)



[Bank of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_India) HQ in [BKC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandra_Kurla_Complex)



India bonds&#x20;

&#x20; 30 year

&#x20; 10 year

&#x20; 2 year

&#x20; 1 year

&#x20; 3 month

The financial services industry contributed \$809 billion (37% of GDP) and employed 14.17 million people (3% of the workforce) in 2016, and the banking sector contributed \$407 billion (19% of GDP) and employed 5.5 million people (1% of the workforce) in 2016.[^cite_note-WTTCBenchmark-250] The Indian [money market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Money_market) is classified into the organised sector, comprising private, public and foreign-owned [commercial banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_bank) and [cooperative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperative) banks, together known as 'scheduled banks'; and the unorganised sector, which includes individual or family-owned indigenous bankers or [money lenders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Money_lending) and [non-banking financial companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-banking_financial_company).[^cite_note-307] The unorganised sector and [microcredit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microcredit) are preferred over traditional banks in rural and sub-urban areas, especially for non-productive purposes such as short-term loans for ceremonies.[^cite_note-Datt-6-308]

India's gross domestic [savings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saving) in 2006–07 as a percentage of GDP stood at a high 32.8%.[^cite_note-309] More than half of personal savings are invested in physical assets such as land, houses, cattle, and gold.[^cite_note-310] The government-owned public-sector banks hold over 75% of total assets of the banking industry, with the private and foreign banks holding 18.2% and 6.5% respectively.[^cite_note-factual-311] Since liberalisation, the government has approved significant banking reforms. While some of these relate to nationalised banks – such as reforms encouraging mergers, reducing government interference and increasing profitability and competitiveness – other reforms have opened the banking and insurance sectors to private and foreign companies.[^cite_note-CIA-292][^cite_note-Datt-8-312]

### Retail

Main article: [Retailing in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retailing_in_India)

See also: [List of bazaars in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_bazaars_in_India), [List of shopping malls in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_shopping_malls_in_India), and [Category\:Indian brands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Indian_brands)



Surat Diamond City Aerial View

The retail industry, excluding wholesale, contributed \$793 billion (10% of GDP) and employed 35 million people (8% of the workforce) in 2020. The industry is the second largest employer in India, after agriculture.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315] The Indian retail market is estimated to be US\$600 billion and one of the top-five retail markets in the world by economic value. India has one of the fastest-growing retail markets in the world,[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317] and is projected to reach \$1.3 trillion by 2020.[^cite_note-pwc12tik-318][^cite_note-319] India has [retail market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retailing_in_India) worth \$1.17 trillion, which contributes over 10% of India's GDP. It also has one of the world's fastest growing [e-commerce](9611.md) markets.[^cite_note-320] The e-commerce retail market in India was valued at \$32.7 billion in 2018, and is expected to reach \$71.9 billion by 2022.[^cite_note-321]

### Services

#### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_India)

For other uses, see [List of tourist attractions in Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tourist_attractions_in_Delhi).



&#x20;Scenic view from [Harangi Elephant Camp & Tree Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harangi_Dam#Amenities) in [Coorg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kodagu)



[Ayodhya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayodhya) became the top destination of [Uttar Pradesh](231623.md) in 2024

The [World Travel & Tourism Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Travel_and_Tourism_Council) calculated that tourism generated ₹15.24 trillion (US\$160 billion) or 9.4% of the nation's GDP in 2017 and supported 41.622 million jobs, 8% of its total employment. The sector is predicted to grow at an annual rate of 6.9% to ₹32.05 trillion (US\$330 billion) by 2028 (9.9% of GDP).[^cite_note-322] The [tourism industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_India) contributes about 9.2% of India's GDP and employs over 42 million people.[^cite_note-323] India earned \$21.07 billion in foreign exchange from tourism receipts in 2015.[^cite_note-324] International tourism to India has seen a steady growth from 2.37 million arrivals in 1997 to 8.03 million arrivals in 2015. [Bangladesh](3454.md) is the largest source of international tourists to India, while European Union nations and Japan are other major sources of international tourists.[^cite_note-unwtoitp-325][^cite_note-326] Over 12 million Indian citizens take international trips each year for tourism, while domestic tourism within India adds about 740 million Indian travellers.[^cite_note-unwtoitp-325]

India has a fast-growing [medical tourism sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_tourism_in_India) of its health care economy, offering low-cost health services and long-term care.[^cite_note-BMJ-327][^cite_note-328] In October 2015, the medical tourism sector was estimated to be worth US\$3 billion. It is projected to grow to \$7–8 billion by 2020.[^cite_note-329] In 2014, 184,298 foreign patients traveled to India to seek medical treatment.[^cite_note-330]

#### Films, entertainment and music industry

Main articles: [Cinema of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_India), [Media of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_India), [Music of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_India), [Animation industry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animation_industry_in_India), and [Video games in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_games_in_India)

The Indian cinema industry is expected to garner a revenue of around Rs 16,198 crore by 2026, of which Rs 15,849 would be Box office revenue and the rest Rs 349 crore from advertising, the report added.[^cite_note-cinema-331] India's Recorded Music industry (which is a key sub-segment) is making steady progress at a CAGR of 13.6 percent, thanks to streaming models.[^cite_note-cinema-331]

## Employment

Main articles: [Labour in India](14120866.md) and [Indian labour law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_labour_law)



India: extreme poverty rates from 1977 to 2022

[^cite_note-332]

As of 2022–23, around 46% of India's workforce was employed in the agricultural sector. This share, which had declined before the pandemic, has risen again in recent years, reaching 46.1% in 2023–24. During the same period, the services sector (excluding agriculture) accounted for about 28.9 percent of employment in 2022–23. The share of manufacturing employment has been more modest 11.4% in 2023–24.[^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334] In 2021–22 and 2022–23, [MoSPI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Statistics_and_Programme_Implementation) reported that the unorganised non-farm sector employed about 19% of India's workforce while contributing only 6% of GDP. Other labour-market estimates indicate that the organised sector accounts for roughly 7% of the total workforce, with the remaining 93% in various forms of unorganised employment.[^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336] Although India's labour productivity is lower than advanced economies, it aligns with levels observed in many emerging Asian countries like China.[^cite_note-337] As of 2023–24, there remains a significant gender gap in employment. In rural areas, 73.5% of working women were self-employed, compared to 59.4% of rural men. In urban areas, 42.3% of women were self-employed versus 39.8% of men. The share of women in regular wage or salaried jobs was only 15.9%. Female labour force participation was 30.5% in rural areas and 20.2% in urban areas, compared to 55.5% and 58.3% for men, respectively. Gender earnings gaps persist: rural female casual labourers earned ₹259 per day, compared to ₹437 per day for rural male casual labourers, although much of this difference may reflect the types of jobs and occupations they engage in rather than direct pay discrimination.[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339]

### Unemployment

Main article: [Unemployment in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment_in_India)

Unemployment in India remains a complex challenge despite signs of modest improvement. According to the Periodic Labour Force Survey (PLFS) for 2024, the joblessness rate for persons aged 15 and above dipped slightly to 4.9%, down from 5.0% in 2023, while the [Labour force participation rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_force_participation_rate) (LFPR) remained broadly stable at about 56.2%. In April 2025, India's first monthly labour-force survey showed an unemployment rate of 5.1%, with an LFPR of 55.6%. However, urban unemployment continues to be more pronounced, reaching around 6.4% in the October–December 2024 quarter, with notably higher rates among women (8.1%) than men (5.8%).[^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342]

Structural issues still persist. A large share of India's workforce is self-employed (57.3%) and many are unpaid helpers in household enterprises (18.3%), which suggests [under-employment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Underemployment) and [disguised employment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disguised_unemployment) remain widespread.[^cite_note-343] Critics also point to limitations in how unemployment is measured. For example, some argue that definitions used by the PLFS count even minimal economic activity (such as one hour of work per week) as "employed", potentially understating the real scale of joblessness.[^cite_note-344]

### Child labour

Main article: [Child labour in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_labour_in_India)

Child labour is a complex problem that is rooted in poverty. Since the 1990s, the government has implemented a variety of programs to eliminate child labour. These have included setting up schools, launching free school lunch programs, creating special investigation cells, etc.[^cite_note-chilab-345][^cite_note-346] Author Sonalde Desai stated that recent studies on child labour in India have found some pockets of industries in which children are employed, but overall, relatively few Indian children are employed. Child labour below the age of 10 is now rare. In the 10–14 age group, the latest surveys find only 2% of children working for wage, while another 9% work within their home or rural farms assisting their parents in times of high work demand such as sowing and harvesting of crops.[^cite_note-347]

### Diaspora remittance

India has the largest diaspora around the world, an estimated 32 million people according to the [Ministry of External Affairs (India)](10302410.md),[^cite_note-348] many of whom work overseas and remit funds back to their families. The Middle East region is the largest source of employment for expat Indians. The crude oil production and infrastructure industry of Saudi Arabia employs over 2 million expat Indians. Cities such as [Dubai](211583.md) and [Abu Dhabi](18950756.md) in United Arab Emirates have employed another 2 million Indians during the construction boom in recent decades.[^cite_note-349]

### Trade unions

Main article: [Trade unions in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_unions_in_India)

In India, the Trade Union movement is generally divided on political lines. According to provisional statistics from the [Ministry of Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Labour_and_Employment_\(India\)), trade unions had a combined membership of 24,601,589 in 2002. As of 2008, there are 12 Central Trade Union Organisations (CTUO) recognized by the Ministry of Labour.[^cite_note-350] The forming of these unions was a big deal in India. It led to a big push for more regulatory laws which gave workers a lot more power.[^cite_note-351]

## Income and consumption

Main article: [Income in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_in_India)



India vs world by GDP per capita adjusted for PPP in 2026

&#x20; India

&#x20; Less than India

&#x20; More than India



India vs world by nominal GDP per capita in 2025

[^cite_note-352]



[Gini index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gini_coefficient) of India compared to other countries per [World Bank](36752.md) data tables as of 2018

[^cite_note-353]

India's [gross national income](1242956.md) per capita had experienced high growth rates since 2002. It has increased from [₹](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_rupee_sign)19,040 in 2002–03 to [₹](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E2%82%B9)1,88,892 in 2024, This translates to approximately [\$](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dollar_sign)2,268.[^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355] India's GNI per capita has seen considerable growth in the last 10 years. While specific average growth rates are not consistently reported, the available data indicates a positive trend. For example, India's GNI per capita increased by 6.72% in 2023, reaching \$2,540. In comparison, the per capita income at constant (2011–12) prices increased by 35.12% between 2014–15 and 2022–23. This indicates a positive trend in both nominal and real terms.[^cite_note-356] The estimated GNI per capita for India in 2025 is \$2,878 (nominal) at current prices, according to the [IMF](15251.md) World Economic Outlook.[^cite_note-357]



Countries by nominal GNI per capita according to the Atlas method (2018)

### Income inequality

Wealth distribution in India is uneven, but less extreme than in some developed countries. As of 2022–23, the top 1% of Indians held about 40.1% of the country's total wealth, while the top 10% accounted for roughly 57–60% of national income.[^cite_note-358] Although wealth is concentrated among the richest segments, India's inequality is lower than in countries such as the United States, Japan, and the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-359] Inequality worsened since the establishment of income tax in 1922, overtaking the British Raj's record of the share of the top 1% in national income, which was 20.7% in 1939–40.[^cite_note-360]

### Poverty

Main article: [Poverty in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_India)



Poverty rate map of India by prevalence in 2012, among its states and union territories

In May 2014, the World Bank reviewed and proposed revisions to its poverty calculation methodology of 2005 and purchasing-power-parity basis for measuring poverty. According to the revised methodology, the world had 872.3 million people below the new poverty line, of which 179.6 million lived in India. With 17.5% of the total world's population, India had a 20.6% share of the world's poorest in 2013.[^cite_note-sdcnk-361] According to a 2005–2006 survey,[^cite_note-362] India had about 61 million children under the age of 5 who were chronically malnourished. A 2011 UNICEF report stated that between 1990 and 2010, India achieved a 45 percent reduction in mortality rates under the age of 5, and now ranks 46th of 188 countries on this metric.[^cite_note-363]

Since the early 1960s, successive governments have implemented various schemes to alleviate poverty, under central planning, that have met with partial success.[^cite_note-survey-364] In 2005, the government enacted the [Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi_National_Rural_Employment_Guarantee_Act) (MGNREGA), guaranteeing 100 days of minimum wage employment to every rural household in all the districts of India.[^cite_note-Panagariya_2008_146-365] In 2011, it was widely criticised and beset with controversy for corrupt officials, deficit financing as the source of funds, poor quality of infrastructure built under the programme, and unintended destructive effects.[^cite_note-wsj1-366][^cite_note-econ1-367][^cite_note-368] Other studies suggest that the programme has helped reduce rural poverty in some cases.[^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370] Yet other studies report that India's economic growth has been the driver of sustainable employment and poverty reduction, though a sizeable population remains in poverty.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372] India lifted 271 million people out of poverty between 2006 and 2016, recording the fastest reductions in the multidimensional poverty index values during the period with strong improvements in areas such as assets, cooking fuel, sanitation, and nutrition.[^cite_note-373]



Graph of selected poverty rates in the 1990s.

On the 2019 [Global Hunger Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Hunger_Index) India ranked 102nd (out of 117 countries) with a score of 30.3, being categorized as 'serious' in severity.[^cite_note-374] In 2024, India ranked 105 (out of 127 countries) with a score of 27.3, indicating an improvement in its hunger situation.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376]

According to the [World Bank](36752.md)'s Spring [2025 Poverty and Equity Brief](https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099722104222534584/pdf/IDU-25f34333-d3a3-44ae-8268-86830e3bc5a5.pdf), approximately 171 million people in India moved out of extreme poverty between 2011 and 2023. During this period, the country's poverty rate declined significantly from 16.2% to 2.3%, based on the international poverty line of \$2.15 per day.[^cite_note-\:0-377]

## Budget, fiscal policy, and taxation

Main articles: [Union budget of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_budget_of_India) and [Taxation in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_India)

See also: [Inflation in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_in_India) and [Foreign aid to India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_aid_to_India)

These paragraphs are an excerpt from [Taxation in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_India).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Taxation_in_India\&action=edit)]

[Taxes in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_India) are levied by the [Central Government](553883.md) and the [State Governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_governments_of_India) by virtue of powers conferred to them from the [Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India).[^cite_note-378] Some minor taxes are also levied by the local authorities such as the Municipality and Panchayats.[^cite_note-379]

The authority to levy a tax is derived from the [Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India) which allocates the power to levy various taxes between the Union Government and the State Governments. An important restriction on this power is Article 265 of the Constitution which states that "No tax shall be levied or collected except by the authority of law".[^cite_note-Taxation_in_India_coi-380] Therefore, each tax levied or collected has to be backed by an accompanying law, passed either by the [Parliament](410238.md) or the [State Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_Assembly). Nonetheless, tax evasion is a massive problem in India, ultimately catalysing various negative effects on the country. In 2023–24, the Direct tax collections reported by CBDT were approximately [₹](490783.md)1,900,000 [crore](121420.md) (equivalent to ₹23 trillion or US\$240 billion in 2026).[^cite_note-381]

## Welfare

Main articles: [Subsidies in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidies_in_India), [Social security in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_security_in_India), [Pensions in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pensions_in_India), and [List of schemes of the government of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_schemes_of_the_government_of_India)

## Currency



Graph of GBP's value against INR \
(1947 to 2025)

The [Indian rupee](490783.md) (₹) is the only [legal tender](267793.md) in India, and is also accepted as legal tender in neighbouring Nepal and Bhutan, both of which peg their currency to that of the Indian rupee. The rupee previously was divided into 100 paise, which no longer exist for public use. The highest-denomination banknote was the ₹2,000 note until 30 September 2023 after which it was scrapped and ₹500 note became the highest denomination; the lowest-denomination coin in circulation is the ₹1 coin.[^cite_note-382] In 2017, demonetisation was announced in which ₹500 and ₹1000 notes were withdrawn and new ₹500 notes were issued. India's monetary system is managed by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI), the country's [central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_bank).[^cite_note-383]

## Security markets

See also: [List of stock exchanges in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_stock_exchanges_in_India), [List of Indian exchange-traded funds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indian_exchange-traded_funds), [Mutual funds in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutual_funds_in_India), [Securities and Exchange Board of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Securities_and_Exchange_Board_of_India), and [Financial regulation in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_regulation_in_India)



The [National Stock Exchange of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Stock_Exchange_of_India) (NSE) is the biggest [stock exchange](28168.md) in India by trading volume as 96% trading occurs in NSE

The development of Indian security markets began with the launch of the [Bombay Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombay_Stock_Exchange) (BSE) in July 1875 and the [Ahmedabad Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmedabad_Stock_Exchange) in 1894. Since then, 22 other exchanges have traded in Indian cities. In 2014, India's stock exchange market became the 10th largest in the world by market capitalisation, just above those of South Korea and Australia.[^cite_note-384] India's two major stock exchanges, BSE and the [National Stock Exchange of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Stock_Exchange_of_India), had a market capitalisation of US\$1.71 trillion and US\$1.68 trillion as of February 2015, according to the [World Federation of Exchanges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Federation_of_Exchanges), which grew to \$3.36 trillion and \$3.31 trillion respectively by September 2021.[^cite_note-bseindia.com-385][^cite_note-386]

The initial public offering (IPO) market in India has been small compared to NYSE and NASDAQ, raising US\$300 million in 2013 and US\$1.4 billion in 2012. [Ernst & Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_&_Young) stated[^cite_note-eygipo-387] that the low IPO activity reflects market conditions, slow government approval processes, and complex regulations. Before 2013, Indian companies were not allowed to list their securities internationally without first completing an IPO in India. In 2013, these security laws were reformed and Indian companies can now choose where they want to list first: overseas, domestically, or both concurrently.[^cite_note-388] Further, security laws have been revised to ease overseas listings of already-listed companies, to increase liquidity for private equity and international investors in Indian companies.[^cite_note-eygipo-387]

### Foreign trade



India's main trading partners in FY 2024–25. For the 2025–26 financial year, India's cumulative exports (merchandise and services) reached approximately US\$863 billion.

[^cite_note-MoCI2026-389]

Until the liberalisation of 1991, India was largely and intentionally isolated from world markets, to protect its economy and to achieve self-reliance. Foreign trade was subject to import tariffs, export taxes and quantitative restrictions, while [foreign direct investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment) (FDI) was restricted by upper-limit equity participation, restrictions on technology transfer, export obligations and government approvals; these approvals were needed for nearly 60% of new FDI in the industrial sector. The restrictions ensured that FDI averaged only around \$200 million annually between 1985 and 1991; a large percentage of the capital flows consisted of foreign aid, commercial borrowing and deposits of [non-resident Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-resident_Indian_and_person_of_Indian_origin).[^cite_note-tn-srinivasan-391] India's exports were stagnant for the first 15 years after independence, due to general neglect of trade policy by the government of that period; imports in the same period, with early industrialisation, consisted predominantly of machinery, raw materials and consumer goods.[^cite_note-392] Since liberalisation, the value of India's international trade has increased sharply,[^cite_note-393] with the contribution of total trade in goods and services to the GDP rising from 16% in 1990–91 to 47% in 2009–10.[^cite_note-Trade-394][^cite_note-WTO_profile-395]

### Balance of payments



[Cumulative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumulativity) current account balance 1980–2008 based on IMF data

Since independence, India's [balance of payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balance_of_payments) on its [current account](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Current_account_\(balance_of_payments\)) has been negative. Since economic liberalisation in the 1990s, precipitated by a balance-of-payment crisis, India's exports rose consistently, covering 80.3% of its imports in 2002–03, up from 66.2% in 1990–91.[^cite_note-396]

### Foreign direct investment

Main article: [Foreign direct investment in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment_in_India)

See also: [List of special economic zones in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_special_economic_zones_in_India) and [International Investment Agreements of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Investment_Agreements_of_India)

As the third-largest economy in the world in PPP terms, India has attracted [foreign direct investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment) (FDI).[^cite_note-financialexpress1-397] During the year 2011, FDI inflow into India stood at \$36.5 billion, 51.1% higher than the 2010 figure of \$24.15 billion. India has strengths in telecommunication, information technology and other significant areas such as auto components, chemicals, apparels, pharmaceuticals, and jewellery. Despite a surge in foreign investments, rigid FDI policies[^cite_note-news.indiamart.com-398] were a significant hindrance. Over time, India has adopted a number of FDI reforms.[^cite_note-financialexpress1-397] India has a large pool of skilled managerial and technical expertise. The size of the [middle-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_of_living_in_India) population stands at 300 million and represents a growing consumer market.[^cite_note-399]

#### Outflows

Since 2000, Indian companies have expanded overseas, investing FDI and creating jobs outside India. From 2006 to 2010, FDI by Indian companies outside India amounted to 1.34 per cent of its GDP.[^cite_note-400] Indian companies have deployed FDI and started operations in United States,[^cite_note-401] Europe and Africa.[^cite_note-402] The Indian conglomerate [Tata Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tata_Group) is United Kingdom's largest manufacturer and private-sector employer.[^cite_note-403][^cite_note-404]

### Remittances

Main article: [Remittances to India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remittances_to_India)

In 2015, a total of US\$68.91 billion was made in remittances to India from other countries, and a total of US\$8.476 billion was made in remittances by foreign workers in India to their home countries. [UAE](69328.md), US, and [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) were the top sources of remittances to India, while Bangladesh, Pakistan, and Nepal were the top recipients of remittances from India.[^cite_note-405] Remittances to India accounted for 3.32% of the country's GDP in 2015.[^cite_note-HDI-406]

## Economic issues

### Corruption

Main articles: [Corruption in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_India) and [Indian black money](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_black_money)



A map depicting [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index) in other countries as compared to India in 2023; a higher score indicates lower levels of corruption&#x20;

&#x20; 100 – 90

&#x20; 89 – 80

&#x20; 79 – 70

&#x20; 69 – 60

&#x20; 59 – 50

&#x20; 49 – 40

&#x20; 39 – 30

&#x20; 29 – 20

&#x20; 19 – 10

&#x20; 9 – 0

&#x20; No data

Corruption has been a pervasive problem in India.[^cite_note-407] A 2005 study by [Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International) (TI) found that more than half of those surveyed had first-hand experience of paying a bribe or peddling influence to get a job done in a public office in the previous year.[^cite_note-2005-TI-study-408] A follow-up study in 2008 found this rate to be 40 percent.[^cite_note-409] In 1996, [red tape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_tape), bureaucracy, and the [Licence Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Licence_Raj) were suggested as a cause for institutionalised corruption and inefficiency.[^cite_note-410] 21st century reports suggest the causes of corruption include excessive regulations and approval requirements, mandated spending programs, monopoly of certain goods and service providers by government-controlled institutions, bureaucracy with discretionary powers, and lack of transparent laws and processes.[^cite_note-kpmg2011-411][^cite_note-WFR2011-412][^cite_note-413]

Computerisation of services, various central and state vigilance commissions, and the 2005 [Right to Information Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_Information_Act) – which requires government officials to furnish information requested by citizens or face punitive action – have considerably reduced corruption and opened avenues to redress grievances.[^cite_note-2005-TI-study-408]

### Overcriminalisation

Main article: [Overcriminalisation in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overcriminalisation_in_India)

The use of criminal punishment in India has expanded indiscriminatory into civil, social, economic, and regulatory affairs becoming a tool for day-to-day governance.[^cite_note-\:3-414] Scholars have noted crude estimations of offences ranging from 12,000 to 150,000.[^cite_note-415] A 2025 report by [Vidhi Centre for Legal Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vidhi_Centre_for_Legal_Policy), a New Delhi-based [think tank](37101.md) estimated that federal laws in India contained 7,305 distinct offences across 370 central laws, out of \~890 that were in force then.[^cite_note-\:3-414] Specifically related to business laws, a 2022 report by [Observer Research Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Observer_Research_Foundation) and [TeamLease Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TeamLease_Services) noted 26,134 criminal clauses in India's business laws, and asserted that of the total 69,233 compliances in India, 37.8 percent carried imprisonment as a punishment for violation.[^cite_note-\:4-200]

### Literacy rates

Main article: [Literacy in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy_in_India)



[University of Calcutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Calcutta), established in 1857, was the first multidisciplinary and secular Western-style institution in Asia.

India has made progress in increasing the primary education attendance rate and expanding [literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy_in_India) to approximately three-fourths of the population.[^cite_note-wbie-416] India's literacy rate had grown from 52.2% in 1991 to 74.04% in 2011. The right to education at the elementary level has been made one of the fundamental rights under the Eighty-Sixth Amendment of 2002, and legislation has been enacted to further the objective of providing free education to all children.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEconomic_Survey2010280–281-417] However, the literacy rate of 74% is lower than the worldwide average, and the country suffers from a high drop-out rate.[^cite_note-elephant-418] Literacy rates and educational opportunities vary by region, gender, urban and rural areas, and among different social groups.[^cite_note-419][^cite_note-420]

### Economic disparities

Main articles: [Economic disparities in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_disparities_in_India), [Poverty in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_India), and [Standard of living in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_of_living_in_India)



Economic disparities among the states and union territories of India, on GDP per capita, [PPP basis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity), and GDP per capita basis in 2011

> &#x20;Poverty rates in India's poorest states are three to four times higher than those in the more advanced states. While India's average annual per capita income was \$1,410 in 2011 – placing it among the poorest of the world's middle-income countries – it was just \$436 in Uttar Pradesh (which has more people than Brazil) and only \$294 in Bihar, one of India's poorest states.

— World Bank: India Country Overview 2013[^cite_note-wboverview-421]

A critical problem facing India's economy is the sharp and growing regional variations among India's different states and territories in terms of poverty, availability of infrastructure, and socio-economic development.[^cite_note-Datt-13-422] Six low-income states – [Assam](186162.md), Chhattisgarh, [Nagaland](408677.md), Madhya Pradesh, [Odisha](250724.md), and [Uttar Pradesh](231623.md) – are home to more than one-third of India's population.[^cite_note-wbstrategy-423] Severe disparities exist among states in terms of income, literacy rates, life expectancy, and living conditions.[^cite_note-424] The four states of Maharashtra, Tamil Nadu, Gujarat and Karnataka alone are projected to account for almost 50% of India's GDP by 2030; the five [South Indian states](678583.md) are projected to contribute 35% of India's GDP by 2030 despite currently having 20% of India's population.[^cite_note-425]

The five-year plans, especially in the pre-liberalisation era, attempted to reduce regional disparities by encouraging industrial development in the interior regions and distributing industries across states. The results have been discouraging as these measures increased inefficiency and hampered effective industrial growth.[^cite_note-426] The more advanced states have been better placed to benefit from liberalisation, with well-developed infrastructure and an educated and skilled workforce, which attract the manufacturing and service sectors. Governments of less-advanced states have tried to reduce disparities by offering tax holidays and cheap land and focused on sectors like tourism, which can develop faster than other sectors.[^cite_note-understanding-2-427][^cite_note-planning-2-428] India's income [Gini coefficient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gini_coefficient) is 33.9, according to the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), indicating overall income distribution to be more uniform than East Asia, Latin America, and Africa.[^cite_note-GINI_index-13] According to a 2021 report by the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center), India has roughly 1.2 billion lower-income individuals, 66 million middle-income individuals, 16 million upper-middle-income individuals, and 2 million in the high-income group.[^cite_note-429] As per *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)*, 78 million of India's population are considered middle class as of 2017, if defined using the cutoff of those making more than \$10 per day, a standard used by the India's [National Council of Applied Economic Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Council_of_Applied_Economic_Research).[^cite_note-teindia2-430]

India has achieved significant reductions in both monetary and multidimensional poverty in recent years. According to the World Bank's 2025 Poverty & Equity Brief, India's extreme poverty rate, measured at the international poverty line of \$2.15 per day (2017 PPP), declined from 16.2% in 2011–12 to 2.3% in 2022–23, lifting approximately 171 million people out of extreme poverty.[^cite_note-431] In terms of [multidimensional poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multidimensional_Poverty_Index), a [United Nations Development Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Development_Programme) (UNDP) report found that India was among 25 countries that halved their global [Multidimensional Poverty Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multidimensional_Poverty_Index) (MPI) values within 15 years (2005–06 to 2019–21), with an estimated 415 million people exiting multidimensional poverty during that period.[^cite_note-432]

### Climate change

Main article: [Climate change in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_India)

See also: [Carbon market in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_market_in_India) and [Conservation in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_in_India)

Several banks including State Bank of India, Axis Bank, ICICI Bank and HDFC Bank have taken the lead in funding green projects even as the Reserve Bank of India comes up with norms for lenders to disclose their actions on climate risk. RBI in an earlier report in 2022, had said that climate change would require "intensive capital mobilisation" for that India needs \$17.77 trillion.[^cite_note-433]
